# CHAPTER 23: LABOR LAW ARCHITECTURE, INDUSTRIAL RELATIONS & THE FOUR NEW LABOR CODES (2020)

**Treatise**: Sovereign Master Notes on Indian Economy & Macroeconomic Architecture  
**Shelf**: 007 (Isolated Master Knowledge HQ)  
**Curriculum Focus**: Economic and Social Issues (ESI) · Labor Market Reforms · 4 New Labor Codes · Social Security Architecture · Gig & Platform Economy · Ease of Doing Business  
**Primary Canonical Sources**: 2nd National Commission on Labour (2002, Ravindra Varma Committee) · Ministry of Labour and Employment Legislative Enactments (Acts No. 29, 35, 36, 37 of 2020) · Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) · NITI Aayog (*Booster for Gig Economy*) · RBI Grade B / NABARD ESI Official Modules (CGB Mentors ESI 2026)  
**Pedagogical Objective**: Definitive, doctoral-depth analysis of India's labor market transformation. Deconstructs the historical pathologies of the pre-reform regime (44+ central and 200+ state statutes, Inspector Raj, enterprise dwarfism), provides an exhaustive comparative breakdown of all Four Labor Codes (Wages, Industrial Relations, Social Security, OSH), details statutory thresholds, evaluates gig worker social protection frameworks, and furnishes examination matrices.

---18:T168e,In the constitutional architecture of India, **Labor is a Concurrent List subject (List III, Seventh Schedule)**:
- **Entry 22**: Trade unions; industrial and labour disputes.
- **Entry 23**: Social security and social insurance; employment and unemployment.
- **Entry 24**: Welfare of labour including conditions of work, provident funds, employers' liability, workmen's compensation, invalidity and old age pensions, and maternity benefits.

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   CONSTITUTIONAL PILLARS OF LABOR RIGHTS & SOCIAL JUSTICE                        │
├────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Constitutional Art │ Canonical Mandate & Constitutional Guarantee                                │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 21**     │ Right to life includes the right to livelihood and human dignity            │
│                    │ (*Bandhua Mukti Morcha v. Union of India, 1984*).                           │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 23**     │ Prohibition of traffic in human beings and forced labor (begar).            │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 24**     │ Prohibition of employment of children below 14 years in factories/mines.     │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 39(d)**  │ Equal pay for equal work for both men and women.                            │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 41**     │ Right to work, to education and to public assistance in cases of             │
│                    │ unemployment, old age, sickness and disablement.                            │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 42**     │ Provision for just and humane conditions of work and maternity relief.      │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 43**     │ Living wage, conditions of work ensuring a decent standard of life.         │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Article 43A**    │ Participation of workers in management of industries (42nd Amendment, 1976).│
└────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### The 2nd National Commission on Labour (2002)
The genesis of the four labor codes lies in the **Second National Commission on Labour (2002)**, chaired by **Ravindra Varma**. The commission concluded that:
1. India's existing labor laws were archaic, contradictory, excessively complex, and unsuited to an open market economy.
2. The prevailing framework of **over 40 central labor acts and 200+ state laws** created overlapping jurisdictions, inspector harassment, and institutional corruption.
3. It recommended consolidating the entire legislative architecture into **four or five broad codes** covering:
   - Industrial Relations
   - Wages
   - Social Security
   - Safety, Health & Working Conditions
   - Welfare

---19:Te84,Prior to the 2020 codification, the Indian labor market suffered from severe structural distortions:

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    PRE-REFORM LABOR DISTORTIONS & THEIR ECONOMIC CONSEQUENCES                    │
├──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Structural Pathology     │ Economic Impact & Distortion Mechanism                                │
├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Enterprise Dwarfism**  │ Under Chapter V-B of the Industrial Disputes Act 1947, firms with     │
│ (*Missing Middle*)       │ ≥ 100 workers required government permission for retrenchment,    │
│                          │ lay-off, or closure. Firms deliberately stayed tiny (<99 workers)     │
│                          │ to avoid regulatory compliance, inhibiting economies of scale.        │
├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Informalization Trap** │ Over **90% of India's workforce** was employed in the informal sector,│
│                          │ completely outside the ambit of statutory minimum wages, provident    │
│                          │ funds, or occupational health safeguards.                             │
├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Contractualization**   │ To bypass Chapter V-B, employers hired contractual labor through third-│
│                          │ party staffing agencies. Contractual workers received significantly   │
│                          │ lower wages for identical work, depressing worker productivity.       │
├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Multiplicity & Fraud** │ Multiple conflicting definitions of "wages", "worker", "employee",    │
│                          │ and "factory" across statutes invited endless litigation and graft.   │
└──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---1a:T1570,The Central Government codified **29 Central Labor Statutes** into **Four Comprehensive Labor Codes**:

```

---1b:T1b0d,### 1. The Code on Wages, 2019
The Code on Wages removes statutory thresholds and extends the right to minimum wages and timely payment to **all 500+ million workers** across both organized and unorganized sectors.

- **Unified Definition of Wages**:
  Consists of three components: Basic Pay, Dearness Allowance (DA), and Retaining Allowance.
  - **The 50% Rule**: Allowances (HRA, conveyance, overtime, bonus) cannot exceed **50% of total compensation**. If allowances exceed 50%, the excess amount is automatically added back to "wages", protecting workers' provident fund (EPF) and gratuity contributions from being artificially diluted by employers.
- **Universal Statutory Minimum Wage**:
  Eliminates the historical distinction between "scheduled employment" and non-scheduled employment. Minimum wages must be paid across all industries.
- **National Floor Minimum Wage**:
  - The Central Government is empowered to fix a statutory **National Floor Minimum Wage**, taking into account regional minimum living standards.
  - State Governments may fix their state minimum wage rates, but **no State can set a minimum wage below the National Floor Minimum Wage**.
  - If a State's existing minimum wage is already higher than the National Floor, the State cannot reduce it.
- **Review Frequency**: Minimum wages must be reviewed and revised at intervals not exceeding **5 years**.
- **Gender Inclusivity**: Prohibits gender discrimination in recruitment and wage payment for identical work.

---

### 2. The Industrial Relations Code, 2020
The IR Code balances workers' collective bargaining rights with employer flexibility to hire and expand.

- **Threshold for Prior Government Permission Raised**:
  - Under Chapter V-B of the legacy Industrial Disputes Act, establishments with 100 or more workers required prior government approval before retrenchment, lay-off, or closure.
  - The IR Code raises this statutory threshold from **100 workers to 300 workers**.
  - Establishments with fewer than 300 workers can lay off or retrench workers without prior government sanction, subject to paying statutory compensation (15 days' wages per completed year of service).
- **Statutory Recognition of Fixed-Term Employment (FTE)**:
  - Employers can hire workers on fixed-term contracts for specified project durations without third-party labor contractors.
  - **Equality of Treatment**: FTE workers are entitled to identical wages, working hours, healthcare allowances, and statutory benefits as permanent employees doing similar work.
  - **Gratuity Concession**: FTE workers are eligible for statutory gratuity on a pro-rata basis if they render service under the contract for a period of **one year** (contrasting with the standard 5-year requirement for permanent workers).
- **Sole Negotiating Union Framework**:
  - To prevent inter-union rivalries, if an establishment has a single trade union with **51% or more membership**, it is recognized as the sole negotiating union.
  - If no union has 51%, a **Negotiating Council** is formed consisting of representatives of trade unions that possess at least **20% membership**.
- **Mandatory 14-Day Notice for Strikes & Lockouts**:
  - Workers across **all industrial establishments** (not just public utilities) are prohibited from going on strike without giving **at least 14 days' notice** (and not more than 60 days before the strike).
  - Strikes are strictly prohibited during the pendency of conciliation proceedings and 7 days after their conclusion.
- **Worker Reskilling Fund**:
  Establishes a statutory fund financed by employers contributing an amount equal to **15 days' last drawn wages** for every retrenched worker. The fund must be utilized to credit funds directly to the worker's bank account within 45 days of retrenchment.

---

### 3. The Code on Social Security, 2020
The Code on Social Security creates an institutional framework to transition from fragmented welfare boards to universal social protection.

- **Statutory Recognition of Gig & Platform Workers**:
  - **Gig Worker**: A person who performs work or participates in a work arrangement and earns from such activities outside of traditional employer-employee relationships.
  - **Platform Worker**: A person engaged in platform work (mediated by an online platform or mobile application, e.g., delivery drivers, ride-hailing drivers).
- **Social Security Fund for Gig & Platform Workers**:
  - Establishes a dedicated National Social Security Fund financed through:
    1. Central and State budgetary grants.
    2. Mandatory contributions from online aggregators (e.g., ride-sharing, food delivery, logistics, e-marketplaces).
  - **Aggregator Contribution Contribution Rate**: Aggregators must contribute **1% to 2% of their annual turnover**, subject to a maximum cap of **5% of the aggregate amount paid or payable to gig and platform workers**.
- **National Social Security Board**:
  Chaired by the Union Minister for Labour and Employment, with representation from central ministries, state governments, aggregators, and gig worker unions.
- **Universal Registration on Central Portal**:
  Statutory mandate for all unorganized, gig, and platform workers to register via Aadhaar on the **e-Shram portal**.

---

### 4. The Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions (OSH) Code, 2020
The OSH Code consolidates disparate safety, hygiene, and working condition standards into a single regulatory code.

- **Revised Factory Threshold**:
  The threshold definition of a "factory" is raised:
  - From 10 to **20 workers** (for premises using electricity).
  - From 20 to **40 workers** (for premises operating without electricity).
- **Contract Labor Threshold**:
  The threshold for applying the Contract Labor provisions is raised from 20 to **50 contract workers**.
- **Women Permitted for Night Shifts**:
  - Women are statutorily permitted to work in **all establishments for all types of work**, including night shifts (between 7:00 PM and 6:00 AM).
  - **Mandatory Safeguards**: Permitted strictly with the woman worker's explicit consent, subject to employer guarantees regarding occupational safety, transport facility from residence, and crèche provisions.
- **Inter-State Migrant Worker Protections**:
  - A worker who moves independently from one state to another and obtains employment earning up to ₹18,000 per month is statutorily classified as an inter-state migrant worker.
  - **Entitlements**: Mandatory appointment letter, annual **journey allowance** to and from their native place paid by the employer, access to the Public Distribution System (PDS) in either the home or host state (ONORC), and free annual medical checkups for workers aged 45+.
- **Single Registration & Pan-India License**:
  Replaces 10+ separate registrations with a single electronic registration, eliminating inspector visits through computerized randomized inspections.

---1c:Te98,Under the Concurrent List, the Labor Codes require both the Central Government and State Governments to frame operational rules before coming into formal, synchronized effect:

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   STATUS OF DRAFT RULES FORMULATION ACROSS THE 4 CODES                           │
├───────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Labor Code                        │ Number of States / UTs That Have Pre-Published Draft Rules   │
├───────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Code on Wages, 2019               │ **31 States / UTs**                                          │
├───────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Industrial Relations Code, 2020   │ **28 States / UTs**                                          │
├───────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Code on Social Security, 2020     │ **28 States / UTs**                                          │
├───────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ OSH & Working Conditions Code     │ **26 States / UTs**                                          │
└───────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Critical Bottlenecks Delaying Full Pan-India Notification:
1. **Federal Harmonization**: Labor is highly sensitive politically. Some industrialized states have hesitated to notify the 300-worker threshold and 14-day strike notice over trade union protests.
2. **The 50% Wage Rule & Take-Home Salary Concerns**: Corporate employers expressed concern that capping allowances at 50% would sharply increase provident fund and gratuity obligations, increasing payroll costs while reducing workers' immediate take-home pay.
3. **Aggregator Welfare Financing Mechanics**: Formulating the precise algorithmic calculation of aggregator contributions (1-2% of turnover vs 5% of payout) required extensive inter-ministerial coordination with the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY).

---1d:T11ff,```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Examination Trap / Ambiguity    │ Canonical Statutory Precision                                  │
├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Who fixes the National Floor    │ **The Central Government**. States can fix their own minimum   │
│ Minimum Wage?                   │ wage, but cannot set it lower than the National Floor.         │
├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ What is the retrenchment        │ **300 workers** (increased from 100 under legacy Ch V-B).      │
│ threshold in the IR Code?       │ Establishments below 300 do not need prior government approval.│
├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Can women work night shifts?    │ **Yes, across all sectors**, provided there is explicit worker │
│                                 │ consent and employer-provided safety, transport, and crèche.   │
├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ What is the gratuity vesting    │ **1 year** on a pro-rata basis (contrasting with 5 years for   │
│ period for fixed-term workers?  │ regular permanent workers).                                    │
├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ What percentage do aggregators  │ **1% to 2% of annual turnover**, capped at **5% of payout** to │
│ contribute to gig social fund?  │ gig/platform workers.                                          │
├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Minimum strike notice period?   │ **14 days mandatory notice** for all industrial establishments;│
│                                 │ flash strikes are statutorily prohibited.                      │
└─────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### High-Yield Descriptive Examination Questions
1. *"The Four Labor Codes balance the twin imperatives of 'Ease of Doing Business' for employers and 'Universal Social Security' for the informal workforce." Critically analyze with reference to statutory provisions.* (UPSC CSE Mains / RBI Grade B Phase 2 ESI)
2. *Examine the statutory framework established by the Code on Social Security, 2020 for gig and platform workers. What are the key operational challenges in enforcing social security contributions from platform aggregators?* (RBI Grade B / NABARD Grade A)
3. *How does the Industrial Relations Code, 2020 alter the dynamics of collective bargaining in India? Discuss the implications of the 300-worker retrenchment threshold and the sole negotiating union clause.* (Civil Services Mains)1e:T4b3,<div style="page-break-before: always;"></div>

# CHAPTER 22: ECONOMIC PLANNING IN INDIA, FIVE-YEAR PLANS HISTORY & NITI AAYOG GOVERNANCE

Covers pre-independence planning initiatives, the operational trajectory of all 12 Five-Year Plans with mathematical growth models, the institutional transition to NITI Aayog, decentralized planning frameworks (Aspirational Districts & Blocks), and high-yield examination analytical matrices.

---1f:T17ff,Economic planning in India was not an impromptu post-1947 construct; it emerged from deep anti-colonial intellectual currents that recognized laissez-faire capitalism under imperial rule as the root cause of chronic de-industrialization, recurrent famines, and capital drain.

### The Institutional Inception of the Planning Commission (1950)
On **March 15, 1950**, the Government of India established the **Planning Commission** through an **extra-constitutional, non-statutory executive resolution**, following the recommendations of the Advisory Planning Board (1946) chaired by K. C. Neogy.
- **Constitutional Anchor**: Although the Planning Commission itself was not mentioned in the Constitution, its mandate derived directly from the **Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP)**, particularly **Article 39(b)** (distribution of ownership and control of material resources to subserve common good) and **Article 39(c)** (operation of the economic system does not result in the concentration of wealth).
- **Executive Architecture**: The Prime Minister served as the ex-officio Chairman. A full-time Deputy Chairman (holding Cabinet Minister rank) managed day-to-day operations with full-time expert members and union ministers as ex-officio members.

### The National Development Council (NDC, 1952)
To provide a federal platform for consulting the states on Five-Year Plans, the **National Development Council (Rashtriya Vikas Parishad)** was constituted on **August 6, 1952**, also via an executive resolution.
- **Composition**: Prime Minister (Chairman), all Union Cabinet Ministers, Chief Ministers of all States, Administrators of Union Territories, and Members of the Planning Commission.
- **Mandate**: Final approving body for Five-Year Plan drafts, review of national socio-economic policies, and recommendation of measures for achieving plan targets.

### Deep Dive into Landmark Strategic Shifts

#### 1. The Harrod-Domar Formulation (First Plan: 1951–56)
The First Plan relied on the Harrod-Domar growth formulation where economic expansion ($g$) is governed by the national savings rate ($s$) divided by the Incremental Capital-Output Ratio ($ICOR$):
$$g = \frac{s}{ICOR}$$
Because capital was scarce, the primary mechanism was mobilizing domestic savings and directing them into high-multiplier agricultural capital assets (dams, canals). The monsoon co-operated, leading to a remarkable 3.6% actual growth rate against a 2.1% target.

#### 2. The Nehru-Mahalanobis Strategy (Second Plan: 1956–61)
Designed by statistician Prasanta Chandra Mahalanobis, the Second Plan marked India's structural pivot toward capital-goods-heavy, import-substituting industrialization.
- **The Core Axiom**: To produce consumer goods in the future, India had to construct machine-making machines today.
- **The Four-Sector Division**:
  1. *Sector $K$*: Capital goods sector (basic steel, heavy chemicals, electricity) — fully managed by the State.
  2. *Sector $C_1$*: Factory-produced consumer goods.
  3. *Sector $C_2$*: Household/cottage-produced consumer goods (promoted to absorb rural labor).
  4. *Sector $C_3$*: Services and agricultural output.
- **Consequences**: Established India's industrial backbone (Bhilai, Durgapur, Rourkela steel plants), but starved the consumer and agricultural sectors of capital, leading to food shortages and inflation in the Third Plan.

#### 3. The Gadgil Formula for Central Assistance (Fourth Plan: 1969)
Prior to the Fourth Plan, Central assistance to state plans was discretionary. In 1969, Deputy Chairman D. R. Gadgil formulated an objective mathematical matrix for distributing Central Plan assistance:
- **Population (1971 Census)**: 60%
- **Per Capita Income (Deviational Formula)**: 20%
- **Tax Effort**: 10%
- **Special Problems (drought, border, flood)**: 10%
*(Later revised as the Pranab Mukherjee-modified Gadgil Formula in 1991: Population 60%, Income Distance 25%, Fiscal Performance 7.5%, Special Problems 7.5%).*

---21:T54d,By the 2000s, structural pathologies in the Planning Commission's operating model made it obsolete in a liberalized, decentralized market economy:

1. **One-Size-Fits-All "Control-Command" Mentality**:
   The Commission dictated state-level capital expenditure through Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS) with rigid conditionalities. A hill state like Himachal Pradesh and a coastal state like Kerala were subjected to identical scheme norms.
2. **Usurpation of Constitutional Federal Finance**:
   The **Finance Commission** is a constitutional body (Article 280) designed to govern Centre-State fiscal devolution. However, the Planning Commission (an executive body) came to control the distribution of **discretionary capital plan grants** under Article 282, reducing the Finance Commission to a secondary role.
3. **Imposition of Bureaucratic Approvals**:
   State Chief Ministers were forced to travel annually to New Delhi to have their state annual plan budgets vetted and approved by Planning Commission members who often lacked grassroots administrative context.
4. **Irrelevance in a Post-1991 Market Economy**:
   When private corporate investment grew to constitute over 75% of Gross Fixed Capital Formation (GFCF), an agency designed to allocate physical targets for state-run factories became fundamentally misaligned with market realities.

---22:T15fa,In his Independence Day speech on August 15, 2014, Prime Minister Narendra Modi announced the abolition of the Planning Commission. On **January 1, 2015**, the Union Cabinet passed a resolution creating the **National Institution for Transforming India (NITI Aayog)**.

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   STRUCTURAL COMPARISON: PLANNING COMMISSION VS. NITI AAYOG                      │
├─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Structural Dimension    │ Planning Commission (1950–2014)   │ NITI Aayog (2015–Present)          │
├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Institutional Character │ Extra-constitutional, top-down    │ Think-tank & policy advisory body; │
│                         │ central planner                   │ catalyst of federal cooperation    │
├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Fund Allocation Power   │ Possessed statutory power to      │ **Zero fund-allocating powers**;   │
│                         │ allocate plan funds to States     │ expenditure handled by Min. of Fin.│
├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Role of States          │ Spectators via NDC meetings;      │ Equal partners in the Governing    │
│                         │ forced to seek plan approvals     │ Council from day one               │
├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Planning Horizon        │ Rigid 5-Year Plans                │ 15-Year Vision, 7-Year Strategy,   │
│                         │                                   │ 3-Year Action Agenda               │
├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Approach                │ Centralized top-down directive    │ Bottom-up, iterative, empirical   │
├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Part-Time Advisors      │ No institutional provision        │ Employs leading academic experts,  │
│                         │                                   │ lateral entrants & fellows         │
└─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Institutional Structure of NITI Aayog
- **Chairperson**: Prime Minister of India.
- **Vice-Chairperson**: Appointed by the PM; enjoys Cabinet Minister rank (Past: Arvind Panagariya, Rajiv Kumar; Present: Suman Bery).
- **Governing Council**: Comprises the Prime Minister, Chief Ministers of all States and Union Territories with legislatures (Delhi, Puducherry, J&K), and Lieutenant Governors of other UTs. This unifies the erstwhile NDC directly into the executive apex.
- **Regional Councils**: Convened to address specific inter-state or regional challenges (e.g., North-Eastern States, Himalayan ecology).
- **Full-Time Members**: Experts of Minister of State rank (e.g., V. K. Saraswat, Ramesh Chand, V. K. Paul).
- **Ex-Officio Members**: Maximum of 4 Union Ministers nominated by the PM (Defense, Home, Finance, Agriculture).
- **Chief Executive Officer (CEO)**: Appointed by the PM for a fixed tenure in the rank of Secretary to the Government of India.
- **Two Core Hubs**:
  1. **Team India Hub**: Serves as the permanent interface between States and the Central Government.
  2. **Knowledge and Innovation Hub**: Builds the institution's think-tank capabilities, data analytics, and research ecosystem.

---23:Tb2a,Discarding the Soviet-era Five-Year Plan cycle, NITI Aayog introduced a multi-tier planning framework designed to balance long-term aspiration with operational agility:

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   NITI AAYOG'S THREE-TIER DEVELOPMENT PLANNING HORIZON                           │
├────────────────────────────────┬────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Planning Tier                  │ Duration   │ Strategic Scope & Functional Focus                 │
├────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **15-Year Vision Document**    │ 15 Years   │ High-level national societal & economic goals;     │
│ (*Vision 2030 / Viksit Bharat*)│            │ aligned with the UN Sustainable Development Goals. │
├────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **7-Year Strategy Document**   │ 7 Years    │ Mid-term conversion of vision into broad policy    │
│ (*Strategy for New India @ 75*)│            │ architecture, structural reforms & sectoral goals. │
├────────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **3-Year Action Agenda**       │ 3 Years    │ Concrete, short-term operational targets, fiscal   │
│ (*Action Agenda 2017–20*)      │            │ allocations, and legislative roadmaps.             │
└────────────────────────────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---24:T15da,### 1. Cooperative & Competitive Federalism
NITI Aayog drives a twin-track federal philosophy:
- **Cooperative Federalism**: Active hand-holding of states through joint working groups, resolving Centre-State bottlenecks, and fostering shared national priorities.
- **Competitive Federalism**: Ranking states on evidence-based, transparent diagnostic indices to incentivize performance improvement through healthy interstate competition.

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   NITI AAYOG'S FLAGSHIP DIAGNOSTIC RANKING INDICES MATRIX                         │
├───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Diagnostic Index              │ Core Measurement Focus & Diagnostic Scope                        │
├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **SDG India Index**           │ Ranks States/UTs across all 17 SDGs; scores 0 to 100             │
│                               │ (Aspirant <50, Performer 50-64, Front Runner 65-99, Achiever 100)│
├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **State Health Index**        │ Evaluates overall performance & incremental change in health     │
│ (*Healthy States, Progressive*)│ outcomes, governance, and key healthcare inputs (with World Bank)│
├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **School Education Quality    │ Evaluates learning outcomes, access, equity, infrastructure,     │
│ Index (SEQI)**                │ and governance processes in primary & secondary schooling.       │
├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Composite Water Management  │ 28 indicators covering groundwater restoration, irrigation,      │
│ Index (CWMI)**                │ watershed management, and urban water supply sustainability.     │
├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Export Preparedness Index   │ Assesses export policy, business ecosystem, export ecosystem,    │
│ (EPI)**                       │ and export performance across coastal, landlocked, & hill states.│
├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **India Innovation Index**    │ Ranks innovation ecosystem using Enabler & Performance pillars   │
│                               │ on the lines of Global Innovation Index (GII).                   │
└───────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### 2. The Aspirational Districts Programme (ADP, 2018)
Launched in **January 2018**, the ADP targets rapid transformation in **112 most backward districts** across India.
- **The 3C Philosophy**:
  1. **Convergence** of Central & State Schemes.
  2. **Collaboration** among Central, State Prabhari Officers, and District Collectors.
  3. **Competition** among districts driven by monthly public delta rankings.
- **Five Core Focus Sectors & Weightages**:
  - *Health & Nutrition*: **30%**
  - *Education*: **30%**
  - *Agriculture & Water Resources*: **20%**
  - *Financial Inclusion & Skill Development*: **10%**
  - *Basic Infrastructure*: **10%**

### 3. The Aspirational Blocks Programme (ABP, 2023)
Building on ADP's success, the ABP was launched in **January 2023** to transform **500 underdeveloped blocks across 329 districts** in 27 States and 4 UTs, preventing intra-district disparities and deepening grassroots governance.

### High-Yield Descriptive Examination Questions
1. *"The transition from the Planning Commission to NITI Aayog reflects a paradigm shift from command-and-control planning to cooperative and competitive federalism." Critically evaluate.* (UPSC CSE / RBI Grade B Phase 2 ESI)
2. *Examine the structural design of the Aspirational Districts Programme (ADP). How does it leverage data-driven governance to overcome administrative inertia in backward regions?* (RBI Grade B / NABARD Grade A)
3. *Analyze the Mahalanobis strategy of the Second Five-Year Plan. To what extent did it succeed in building an industrial base, and what were its unintended macroeconomic trade-offs?* (Civil Services Mains)26:T558,<div style="page-break-before: always;"></div>

# CHAPTER 24: URBANIZATION, DEMOGRAPHIC TRANSITION & INTERNAL MIGRATION DYNAMICS

**Treatise**: Sovereign Master Notes on Indian Economy & Macroeconomic Architecture  
**Shelf**: 007 (Isolated Master Knowledge HQ)  
**Curriculum Focus**: Economic and Social Issues (ESI) · Urbanization Dynamics · Internal Migration Patterns · Census 2011 Urban Metrics · Push-Pull Models · Urban Governance Missions (Smart Cities, AMRUT 2.0, PMAY-U, ONORC)  
**Primary Canonical Sources**: Census of India (2011, Office of the Registrar General & Census Commissioner) · Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA Annual Reports & Mission Guidelines) · Economic Survey of India (*Internal Migration Cohorts*) · NITI Aayog (*Reforms in Urban Planning Capacity in India*) · RBI Grade B / NABARD ESI Official Modules (CGB Mentors ESI 2025/2026)  
**Pedagogical Objective**: Complete, doctoral-depth analysis of India's spatial and demographic transition. Deconstructs statutory definitions (Statutory Towns vs Census Towns), examines Census 2011 metrics and state-level variations, details theoretical models of migration (Everett Lee, Ravenstein, Harris-Todaro), analyzes internal circular migration streams, and provides comprehensive operational breakdowns of all flagship urban missions and migrant welfare architecture.

---27:T13b7,The Census of India defines an urban area through strict statutory and demographic criteria, establishing an institutional dichotomy between **Statutory Towns** and **Census Towns**:

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   CENSUS OF INDIA: CLASSIFICATION OF URBAN SETTLEMENTS                           │
├────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Urban Category     │ Canonical Definition & Mandatory Statutory Criteria                         │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Statutory     │ All administrative places governed by a statutory urban local body,        │
│    Towns (ST)**    │ such as a Municipal Corporation (Nagar Nigam), Municipality (Nagar Palika), │
│                    │ Cantonment Board, or Notified Town Area Committee (NTAC).                   │
│                    │ (*Census 2011 count: 4,041 towns*).                                         │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **2. Census        │ Places that are administratively rural (governed by Gram Panchayats) but    │
│    Towns (CT)**    │ simultaneously satisfy **all three** demographic conditions:                │
│                    │ 1. Minimum population of **5,000 persons**;                                 │
│                    │ 2. At least **75% of the male main working population** engaged in         │
│                    │    non-agricultural pursuits;                                               │
│                    │ 3. Population density of at least **400 persons per sq. km** (1,000/sq. mi).│
│                    │ (*Census 2011 count: 3,894 towns — a 186% surge from 1,362 in 2001!*).      │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **3. Outgrowths    │ A viable unit such as a village or part of a village contiguous to a town   │
│    (OG)**          │ possessing urban characteristics (railway colony, university campus, port). │
│                    │ (*Census 2011 count: 981 outgrowths*).                                      │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **4. Urban         │ A continuous urban spread consisting of a town and its adjoining urban      │
│    Agglomeration   │ outgrowths, or two or more physically contiguous towns.                     │
│    (UA)**          │ *Mandatory Rule*: Must consist of at least one statutory town and have a    │
│                    │ total population of not less than **20,000 persons**.                       │
└────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Census City Classification by Population Size
- **Class I Cities**: Population ≥ 100,000 (1 Lakh+).
- **Class II Towns**: Population between 50,000 and 99,999.
- **Class III Towns**: Population between 20,000 and 49,999.
- **Class IV Towns**: Population between 10,000 and 19,999.
- **Class V Towns**: Population between 5,000 and 9,999.
- **Class VI Towns**: Population $< 5,000$.
- **Million-Plus Cities / UAs**: Population ≥ 1,000,000 (10 Lakh+). (*53 cities in Census 2011, up from 35 in 2001*).
- **Mega Cities**: Population ≥ 10,000,000 (1 Crore+). (*Greater Mumbai, Delhi, and Kolkata*).

---28:T1c3b,In the 2011 Census, India recorded an aggregate urban population of **377.1 million (31.16%)**, up from 286.1 million (27.81%) in 2001.

### Components of Urban Growth (2001–2011)
The 91 million increase in India's urban population was driven by three primary components:
1. **Natural Increase** (Births minus Deaths): Accounted for **~44%** of urban growth.
2. **Reclassification of Rural Areas into Census Towns**: Accounted for **~30%** of growth.
3. **Net Rural-to-Urban Migration**: Accounted for **~22%** of growth.
4. **Jurisdictional Boundary Expansions**: Accounted for **~4%** of growth.

---29:Tf75,India's urbanization exhibits a distinct spatial structural pathology known as **Top-Heavy Urbanization** or **Macrocephaly**:
- More than **70% of the total urban population** resides in Class-I cities (100,000+ population).
- The 53 million-plus cities account for **42.6% of the total urban population**.
- Secondary and tertiary towns (Class III to Class VI) suffer from chronic fiscal starvation, infrastructure deficit, and stagnant employment generation, forcing migrants to bypass intermediate centers and crowd directly into Tier-1 megacities.

---2a:Te8c,### 1. Everett Lee's Push-Pull Model of Migration (1966)
Everett Lee conceptualized migration as driven by factors associated with four analytical domains:

```
                  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                  │          EVERETT LEE'S PUSH-PULL DYNAMICS    │
                  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                                         │
        ┌────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┐
        ▼                                                               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────┐                             ┌──────────────────────────────┐
│       ORIGIN (PUSH)          │    INTERVENING OBSTACLES    │     DESTINATION (PULL)       │
│ • Agricultural distress      │ ─── (Distance, physical ───►│ • Higher nominal wages       │
│ • Disguised unemployment     │      barriers, costs,       │ • Modern manufacturing/IT    │
│ • Environmental degradation  │      language, legal        │ • Quality education/health   │
│ • Caste oppression / violence│      restrictions)          │ • Social anonymity & liberty │
└──────────────────────────────┘                             └──────────────────────────────┘
                                         ▲
                                         │
                         PERSONAL FACTORS (Age, Gender, Skills)
```

### 2. E. G. Ravenstein's Laws of Migration (1885)
1. **Distance Decay**: Most migrants move only a short distance; long-distance migrants head preferentially to great centers of commerce and industry.
2. **Step Migration**: Expansion proceeds step-by-step from rural hamlets to market towns, then to secondary cities, and finally to metropolises.
3. **Counter-Streams**: Every major migration stream produces a compensating counter-stream (return migration).
4. **Gender Differentials**: Females are more migratory than males over short distances (dominated by marriage), but males predominate over longer inter-state distances for economic employment.
5. **Technology & Economic Gravity**: Development of transport infrastructure and industrialization accelerates migration volumes.

### 3. The Harris-Todaro Model of Rural-Urban Migration (1970)
John Harris and Michael Todaro formulated an equilibrium model explaining why rural-urban migration continues to surge in developing nations despite massive urban unemployment:
- **The Core Axiom**: Migration is an economic decision governed by **expected wages**, not actual current wages.
- **The Expected Wage Formulation**:
  $$E(W_u) = W_u \times P_e$$
  Where $W_u$ is the urban statutory/formal wage, and $P_e$ is the probability of securing an urban formal job (inversely related to the urban unemployment rate):
  $$P_e = \frac{E_u}{L_u}$$
  *(Ratio of urban employment $E_u$ to total urban labor force $L_u$).*
- **Policy Implication**: Creating jobs in urban areas without raising rural productivity simply induces more rural migrants to flood into the city, paradoxically **increasing urban unemployment** (The Todaro Paradox).

---2b:Tdb6,According to the **2011 Census**, there were **455.7 million internal migrants** in India, representing **37.6% of the country's total population** (up from 314.5 million / 31.5% in 2001).

### Major Inter-State Migration Corridors
Research in the *Economic Survey 2016–17* utilizing railway unreserved passenger travel data (Cohort-based Migration Metric - CMM) revealed annual interstate migration flows of **~9 million people**, nearly double the estimates of Census 2001:
- **Major Out-Migrant Source States**: Uttar Pradesh, Bihar, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, West Bengal.
- **Major In-Migrant Destination States**: Maharashtra, National Capital Territory of Delhi, Gujarat, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Kerala, Punjab.

  * Final Goods vs. Intermediate Goods (The double-counting hazard).
  * Consumption Goods vs. Capital Goods (Gross Investment, Depreciation, Net Investment).
  * Excludability & Rivalry Matrix: Pure Public Goods, Private Goods, Common Pool Resources, Club Goods.

  * Factor Cost ($FC$) ⟶ Basic Price ($BP$) ⟶ Market Price ($MP$).
  * Disentangling Product Taxes/Subsidies from Production Taxes/Subsidies.
  * Headline GDP transition from GDP at Factor Cost to GDP at Market Price.
  * The Role of MCA-21 Database.

* **2.5 Price Adjustments**: Nominal GDP, Real GDP, and the GDP Deflator (vs. CPI).
