---a5:T558,```
CARD 1: Explain the mathematical composition of the Divisible Pool under Article 270 and identify the three statutory exclusions.
ANSWER:
Divisible Pool = Gross Tax Revenue (GTR) of the Centre minus:
1. Surcharges levied under Article 271 for Central purposes;
2. All Cesses levied for specific statutory objectives;
3. Statutory Cost of Tax Collection.
The remaining net proceeds are shared between Union and States (currently 41% to States).
```

```
CARD 2: Detail the horizontal devolution formula of the 15th Finance Commission with exact percentage weights.
ANSWER:
1. Income Distance: 45.0% (Equity)
2. Population (2011 Census): 15.0% (Scale/Need)
3. Area: 15.0% (Cost disability)
4. Forest and Ecology: 10.0% (Ecological preservation)
5. Demographic Performance: 12.5% (Reward for TFR control)
6. Tax Effort: 2.5% (Fiscal efficiency)
Total: 100%
```

```
CARD 3: Under what constitutional condition can the Central Government veto or restrict a State's market borrowing?
ANSWER:
Under Article 293(3), if a State has any outstanding loan from the Central Government or if the
Centre has guaranteed any loan for the State, the State cannot borrow from the market without
prior consent of the Government of India. Since all Indian States have outstanding central loans,
the Centre sets annual Net Borrowing Ceilings (typically 3% of GSDP) for every State.
```a6:T1055,Inflation is defined as a **persistent and generalized increase in the overall price level of goods and services in an economy over a sustained period**, resulting in a continuous erosion of the purchasing power of money.

$$\text{Purchasing Power of Money} = \frac{1}{\text{Price Level } (P)}$$

> **The Precision Filter**: A price spike in a single commodity (e.g., tomatoes following a seasonal flood) is **not** inflation; it is a relative price shock. Inflation requires price escalation across a wide, representative basket over successive accounting intervals.

---

### Classification by Velocity / Rate of Escalation:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
│ Inflation Velocity              │ Annual Rate                   │ Economic Behavior & Manifestation │
├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Creeping Inflation**          │ **1% to 3% per annum**        │ Gentle, predictable. Regarded as  │
│                                 │                               │ essential lubrication for economic│
│                                 │                               │ growth, stimulating investment.   │
├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Walking / Trotting Inflation**│ **3% to 9% per annum**        │ Noticeable price rises. Prompts   │
│                                 │                               │ central banks to intervene before │
│                                 │                               │ inflation expectations unmoor.    │
├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Running / Galloping Inflation**│ **10% to 20% (or double digit)│ Severe disruption. Money loses its│
│                                 │ per annum**                   │ function as a store of value; wage│
│                                 │                               │ contracts break down.             │
├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Hyperinflation**              │ **$> 50\%$ per month**        │ Complete collapse of confidence in│
│                                 │ (Phillip Cagan Definition)    │ sovereign fiat currency. Barter or│
│                                 │ (Thousands/millions % a year) │ foreign currency replaces domestic│
│                                 │                               │ cash (e.g., Weimar 1923,          │
│                                 │                               │ Zimbabwe 2008, Venezuela 2018).   │
└─────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘
```

---a7:T967,### 1. Demand-Pull Inflation
Originates when Aggregate Demand ($AD$) exceeds Aggregate Supply ($AS$) at or near the economy's full-employment potential capacity:

$$\mathbf{AD > AS} \implies \text{Prices Pulled Upward}$$

* **Key Drivers**:
  * Loose, expansionary monetary policy (excessive money supply growth $\Delta M > \Delta Y$).
  * Fiscal profligacy (unbridled deficit financing, direct cash stimulus).
  * Rapid export expansion or sudden surge in domestic private consumption.

```
Price Level
     ▲
     │             AS (Aggregate Supply)
     │            /
  P₂ ┼───────────/────╭─────────── AD₂
     │          /     │
  P₁ ┼─────────/──────┼─── AD₁
     │        /       │
     └───────┴────────┴────────► Real Output (Y)
            Y₁       Y* (Full Capacity)
```

---

### 2. Cost-Push Inflation
Originates from an exogenous reduction in Aggregate Supply caused by an increase in input costs, shifting the supply curve inward (leftward) while aggregate demand remains constant:

$$\text{Input Costs } \uparrow \implies \text{Supply Curve Shifts Left} \implies \text{Prices Rise and Output Contracts}$$

* **Key Drivers**:
  * Global commodity price shocks (e.g., crude oil spikes, fertilizer price jumps).
  * Wage-push pressure (labor unions demanding wages above productivity gains).
  * Currency depreciation (importing raw materials at significantly higher rupee costs).
  * Natural disasters, droughts, and geopolitical blockades.

---

### 3. Structural Inflation (The Latin American / Indian Structuralist School)
Pioneered by Gunnar Myrdal, Raúl Prebisch, and Indian structural economists:
* Argues that inflation in developing economies is not merely a monetary phenomenon, but is driven by **deep-seated institutional bottlenecks and structural rigidities**:
  1. **Agricultural Supply Inelasticity**: Inelastic farm output and storage deficits cause severe food inflation whenever income rises.
  2. **Infrastructure Deficits**: Transport bottlenecks, high logistics costs (railway freight cross-subsidization), and power shortages.
  3. **Foreign Exchange Bottlenecks**: Inelastic import needs (oil, electronics, defense hardware) force currency depreciation, transmitting imported price shocks.

---a8:T1799,```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Specialized Metric              │ Precise Economic Definition & Mechanism                     │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Headline Inflation**          │ Inflation measured across the **entire price index basket** │
│                                 │ (including food, fuel, commodities). Highly volatile.      │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Core Inflation**              │ Inflation measured **excluding volatile components**:       │
│                                 │ $$\mathbf{\text{Core}} = \text{Headline} - [\text{Food} + \text{Fuel}]$$ │
│                                 │ Isolates sticky, underlying demand-driven price pressures.  │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Reflation**                   │ A deliberate, controlled policy-induced price rise initiated│
│                                 │ by the state (via fiscal stimulus or rate cuts) to pull an  │
│                                 │ economy out of a slump, recession, or deflationary trap.   │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Disinflation**                │ A **decrease in the rate of inflation**. Prices are still   │
│                                 │ rising, but at a progressively slower velocity (e.g., if    │
│                                 │ inflation drops from 7% in Year 1 to 4% in Year 2).         │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Deflation**                   │ A **persistent decline in the general price level**         │
│                                 │ (negative inflation rate, $\pi < 0$). Severe hazard: causes │
│                                 │ consumers to postpone purchases, triggering depression.    │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Stagflation**                 │ The toxic coexistence of **stagnant economic growth / high  │
│                                 │ unemployment** alongside **high persistent inflation**      │
│                                 │ (violates the classical trade-off of the Phillips Curve).   │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Shrinkflation**               │ A stealth form of inflation where the **market retail price │
│                                 │ of a product remains unchanged**, but the manufacturer      │
│                                 │ **reduces the package size, volume, weight, or quantity**.  │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Skewflation**                 │ Sustained price inflation concentrated in **one or a few    │
│                                 │ specific sectors** (e.g., pulses or onions) while other     │
│                                 │ commodity prices remain completely stable or depressed.    │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Greedflation**                │ Inflation driven by corporations exploiting general supply  │
│                                 │ disruption narratives to raise profit markups far above     │
│                                 │ underlying input cost increases.                            │
└─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---a9:T957,Originated by A.W. Phillips (1958) and expanded by Paul Samuelson and Robert Solow:

### 1. The Short-Run Phillips Curve (SRPC):
* Hypothesizes a stable, **inverse relationship between the rate of inflation and the rate of unemployment**:

$$\mathbf{\text{Unemployment } \downarrow} \implies \mathbf{\text{Labor Market Tightens}} \implies \mathbf{\text{Wages } \uparrow} \implies \mathbf{\text{Inflation } \uparrow}$$

```
Inflation Rate (π)
       ▲
       │        SRPC
       │        \
    π₁ ┼─────────\
       │          \
    π₂ ┼───────────\
       │            \
       └────────┴────┴────────► Unemployment Rate (u)
               u₁    u₂
```

---

### 2. The Long-Run Breakdown: Friedman-Phelps & NAIRU
During the 1970s OPEC oil shocks, economies experienced simultaneously rising inflation and surging unemployment (**Stagflation**), completely demolishing the stable SRPC.
* **Milton Friedman & Edmund Phelps** (Adaptive Expectations):
  * Workers adjust their inflation expectations ($\pi^e$). Once higher inflation is anticipated, workers demand higher nominal wages, restoring unemployment back to its baseline level.
  * In the Long Run, the Phillips Curve is a **vertical straight line** at the **Non-Accelerating Inflation Rate of Unemployment (NAIRU)** or Natural Rate of Unemployment ($u^*$):

$$\text{Long-Run Phillips Curve (LRPC) is Vertical at } \mathbf{u^*}$$

```
Inflation Rate (π)
       ▲              LRPC (Vertical at NAIRU)
       │                 │
    π₃ ┼─────────────────┼───────── SRPC (Higher Exp)
       │                 │
    π₂ ┼─────────────────┼───────── SRPC (Medium Exp)
       │                 │
    π₁ ┼─────────────────┼───────── SRPC (Low Exp)
       │                 │
       └─────────────────┴────────► Unemployment Rate (u)
                        u* (NAIRU)
```

> **Takeaway**: Central banks cannot permanently reduce unemployment below NAIRU by inflating the economy; attempts to do so result solely in accelerating inflation without lasting employment gains.

```

---

### Why the RBI Switched from WPI to CPI (Combined) in 2014:
Following the recommendations of the **Dr. Urjit Patel Committee (2014)**, the Reserve Bank abandoned WPI and adopted **CPI (Combined)** as its nominal anchor for Flexible Inflation Targeting:
1. **Omission of the Services Sector**: Services account for more than 50% of India's GDP, but WPI captures zero services. WPI completely excluded education, hospital bills, transit, and rents.
2. **Citizen Reality**: Consumers do not buy goods at wholesale mandi rates or factory gates; they buy at local retail shops, paying retail markups and local indirect taxes.
3. **Food Weight**: Food forms a major share of an average Indian citizen's consumption basket (45.86% in CPI vs. 24.38% total food in WPI). An inflation anchor must reflect the cost of living of the vulnerable.

---

### The Labour Bureau Indices
While NSO compiles national headline CPI (Rural, Urban, Combined), the **Labour Bureau** (Ministry of Labour and Employment, Shimla) compiles three specialized segment indices:
1. **CPI-IW (Industrial Workers)**: Base Year updated to **2016 = 100** (previously 2001). Used to calculate **Dearness Allowance (DA)** for Central Government employees and pensioners.
2. **CPI-AL (Agricultural Labourers)**: Base Year **1986–87 = 100**. Used for wage revisions under MGNREGA.
3. **CPI-RL (Rural Labourers)**: Base Year **1986–87 = 100**.

---ad:T63f,```
CARD 1: Define Core Inflation and state why the RBI focuses on Headline CPI rather than Core CPI for its target.
ANSWER:
1. Core Inflation = Headline Inflation minus volatile Food and Fuel items.
2. Why RBI targets Headline CPI: Food and beverages make up 45.86% of the consumer basket.
Food price inflation severely impacts household inflation expectations and living standards in India.
Ignoring food would disconnect the central bank's policy target from the lived reality of the populace.
```

```
CARD 2: Contrast the Short-Run Phillips Curve with the Long-Run Phillips Curve (Friedman-Phelps thesis).
ANSWER:
1. Short-Run: Predicts an inverse trade-off between inflation and unemployment (lower unemployment
requires accepting higher inflation).
2. Long-Run: Workers and firms adjust expectations (adaptive/rational expectations). Unemployment
inevitably returns to the Natural Rate of Unemployment (NAIRU). The Long-Run Phillips Curve is
a vertical line at NAIRU, meaning monetary expansion generates higher inflation without lowering unemployment.
```

```
CARD 3: Detail the three key structural differences between the Consumer Price Index (Combined) and the Wholesale Price Index (WPI).
ANSWER:
1. Scope: CPI covers both Goods and Services; WPI covers Goods ONLY (zero services).
2. Basket Dominance: CPI is dominated by Food and Beverages (45.86%); WPI is dominated by
Manufactured Products (64.23%).
3. Institutional Origin: CPI is published monthly by NSO (MoSPI, Base 2012); WPI is published
monthly by Office of Economic Adviser, DPIIT, Ministry of Commerce (Base 2011–12).
```ae:T5cc,To analyze labor markets with macroeconomic precision, an entire national population must be systematically dissected into mutually exclusive cohorts:

```
                                    TOTAL POPULATION
                                           │
         ┌─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┐
         ▼                                                                   ▼
[WORKING-AGE POPULATION]                                            [DEPENDENT POPULATION]
(Ages 15 to 59 / 64 years)                                          (Children 0–14 & Elderly 60+)
         │
         ├─────────────────────────────────┐
         ▼                                 ▼
   [LABOUR FORCE (LF)]           [OUT OF LABOUR FORCE (OLF)]
   (Actively working OR          (Students, Homemakers, Retired,
    seeking employment)           Discouraged Workers not seeking work)
         │
         ├─────────────────────────────────┐
         ▼                                 ▼
   [EMPLOYED (E)]                  [UNEMPLOYED (U)]
   (Engaged in economic            (Seeking work, available,
    gainful activity)               but cannot find employment)
```

---af:T591,| Indicator | Mathematical Formulation | Denominator Check & Epistemic Focus |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Labour Force Participation Rate (LFPR)** | $$\text{LFPR} = \frac{\text{Labour Force (Employed + Unemployed)}}{\text{Total Working-Age Population (15+)}} \times 100$$ | Measures the proportion of working-age citizens willing and available to work. |
| **Worker Population Ratio (WPR) / Employment Rate** | $$\text{WPR} = \frac{\text{Total Employed Persons}}{\text{Total Population (or 15+)}} \times 100$$ | Measures the proportion of the population actively engaged in productive economic output. |
| **Unemployment Rate (UR)** | $$\text{UR} = \frac{\text{Unemployed Persons}}{\text{Labour Force (E + U)}} \times 100$$ | **CRITICAL DENOMINATOR TRAP**: The denominator is the **Labour Force**, NOT the total population or working age! |
| **Age Dependency Ratio** | $$\text{Dependency Ratio} = \frac{\text{Pop (0–14)} + \text{Pop (60+)}}{\text{Working-Age Pop (15–59)}} \times 100$$ | Measures the economic burden borne by the working-age group. |

> **The Demographic Dividend**: A transient economic window of opportunity occurring when a nation's **Age Dependency Ratio drops to its historical minimum**, meaning the working-age population expands significantly faster than the dependent cohort. In India, this window opened around 2005–06 and will peak near 2041 before transitioning into population aging.

---b0:T28a9,### 1. The Theory of Demographic Transition (Thompson & Notestein Framework)
Human societies systematically transition through distinct demographic stages as economic modernization unfolds:

```
┌─────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────┬─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Stage   │ Phase Character      │ Birth Rate  │ Death Rate  │ Natural Growth & Economic Dynamics                      │
├─────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **I**   │ **High Stationary**  │ High        │ High        │ • **Near Zero Growth**: Primitive agrarian economy,     │
│         │                      │ (35–45/1000)│ (35–45/1000)│   frequent famines, epidemics, zero modern healthcare.   │
│         │                      │             │             │ • *India Pre-1921*: 1921 is the **"Year of the Great    │
│         │                      │             │             │   Divide"**—the only census with negative growth (-0.31%).│
├─────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **II**  │ **Early Expanding**  │ High        │ Rapidly     │ • **Population Explosion**: Sanitation, antibiotics,     │
│         │                      │             │ Falling     │   and famine relief collapse mortality while fertility  │
│         │                      │             │             │   norms remain culturally high.                         │
│         │                      │             │             │ • *India 1921–1971*: Population surged from 251M to 548M│
├─────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **III** │ **Late Expanding**   │ Falling     │ Low & Stable│ • **Decelerating Growth**: Urbanization, female literacy│
│         │                      │             │ (6–8/1000)  │   access to family planning, rising cost of children.   │
│         │                      │             │             │ • *India Today*: National Total Fertility Rate (TFR)    │
│         │                      │             │             │   has dropped to **2.0** (below replacement level 2.1). │
├─────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **IV**  │ **Low Stationary**   │ Low         │ Low         │ • **Stationary / Stable**: Replacement level fertility, │
│         │                      │ (~10/1000)  │ (~10/1000)  │   aging workforce (e.g., Western Europe, Japan).        │
├─────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **V**   │ **Contracting**      │ Below Death │ Stable /    │ • **Natural Population Decline**: Negative growth, severe│
│         │                      │ Rate        │ Rising Age  │   dependency burdens (e.g., Japan, Italy, South Korea). │
└─────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────────┴─────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

### 2. India's Demographic Dividend Window (2018–2055)
* **The Opportunity Window**: India entered the demographic dividend window in 2018 (when the working-age cohort crossed 50% of the population) and it will remain open until approximately **2055** (~37-year window).
* **Comparative Median Age Advantage**:
  - India: **28.4 Years** (youngest large economy in the world).
  - China: 38.4 Years | United States: 38.5 Years | Western Europe: 44.5 Years | Japan: 48.6 Years.
* **The Regional Demographic Asymmetry (The North-South Divide)**:
  - **Southern & Western States (Stage 4)**: Kerala (TFR 1.8), Tamil Nadu (1.8), Andhra Pradesh (1.7), Maharashtra (1.7) have completed the transition. Facing rapid aging, school closures, rising elder dependency, and heavy reliance on interstate migrant labor.
  - **Northern & Eastern States (Late Stage 2 / Early Stage 3)**: Bihar (TFR 3.0), Uttar Pradesh (2.4), Jharkhand (2.3), Meghalaya (2.9) contain the bulk of India's future labor force additions.
  - **The Economic Challenge**: The young labor force is concentrated in states with historically lower fiscal capacity, educational quality, and industrial capital investment.

---

### 3. The "Demographic Dividend" vs. "Demographic Disaster" Matrix
The demographic dividend is not an automatic windfall; it is merely a **demographic potential** that translates into economic growth only through three policy channels:

```
┌───────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Dividend Transmission Mechanism       │ Risk / Structural Failure Trap ("Demographic Disaster")                     │
├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Labor Supply Channel**           │ **Jobless Growth**: Inability to create formal, productive non-farm jobs    │
│    Surging working-age cohort         │ traps youth in disguised unemployment or low-wage gig economy.              │
├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **2. Capital Accumulation Channel**   │ **Skill Mismatch**: Less than 5% of India's workforce has formal vocational │
│    Lower dependency ratio increases   │ training (vs. 75% in Germany, 96% in South Korea), keeping wages stagnant.  │
│    household savings and investment   │                                                                             │
├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **3. Human Capital & FLFPR Channel**  │ **Missing Women**: Subdued Female Labor Force Participation (~37% in PLFS)   │
│    Smaller family size allows deeper  │ forfeits half the demographic potential. India risks "growing old before    │
│    education/health spending per child│ it grows rich".                                                             │
└───────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

### 4. Sex Ratio Economics & The "Missing Women" Phenomenon
* **The Amartya Sen Formulation**: First identified in 1990, the term "Missing Women" quantifies the deficit of females relative to the biologically expected female-to-male ratio, resulting from sex-selective abortion (female foeticide), female infanticide, and differential mortality due to biased nutritional/medical care.
* **Economic Metrics**:
  - **Overall Sex Ratio (Census 2011)**: 940 females per 1,000 males.
  - **Child Sex Ratio (0–6 years)**: Declined from 976 (1961) → 945 (1991) → **918 (2011)**, signaling intense son-preference enabled by ultrasound technology despite the *PCPNDT Act, 1994*.
  - **NFHS-5 (2019–21) Improvement**: Overall sex ratio rose to 1,020 (though Sex Ratio at Birth remains skewed at **929**).
* **Macroeconomic Cost**: IMF and World Bank studies indicate that persistent gender gaps in education, nutrition, and workforce participation suppress India's potential GDP growth rate by **1.5% to 2.5% annually**.

---b1:Tdc2,Historically developed by the National Sample Survey Office (NSSO) and standardized under the modern Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS), employment is categorized across different reference accounting windows:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
│ Approach                        │ Reference Period              │ Activity Determination Rule       │
├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Usual Principal Status        │ **365 Days** (1 Year)         │ Activity on which a person spent  │
│ (UPS)**                         │                               │ the **majority of days ($\ge 183$ │
│                                 │                               │ days)** during the reference year.│
├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Usual Principal and           │ **365 Days** (1 Year)         │ Person is treated as employed if  │
│ Subsidiary Status (UPSS)**      │                               │ they worked in a **subsidiary     │
│ *(Headline Annual Measure)*     │                               │ capacity for at least 30 days**   │
│                                 │                               │ during the year, even if their    │
│                                 │                               │ Principal Status was unemployed/OLF│
├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Current Weekly Status         │ **7 Days** (1 Week)           │ A person is treated as employed   │
│ (CWS)**                         │                               │ if they worked for **at least 1   │
│ *(Short-term / Urban Measure)*  │                               │ hour on ANY ONE DAY** during the  │
│                                 │                               │ reference 7-day week!             │
└─────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘
```

* **Why UPSS Unemployment is always lower than CWS Unemployment**: Under UPSS, any individual who engaged in casual labor or agricultural weeding for even 30 days over 365 days is classified as **employed**. CWS captures short-term seasonality and intermittent joblessness that UPSS masks.

┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Type of Unemployment            │ Theoretical Mechanism & Practical Manifestation             │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Disguised Unemployment**   │ **Marginal Productivity of Labor is ZERO ($\mathbf{MP_L = 0}$)**│
│    *(Hidden Unemployment)*      │ More workers are engaged in an economic activity than are   │
│                                 │ technically required. If surplus workers are completely     │
│                                 │ withdrawn, **Total Output does NOT decline**!               │
│                                 │ • Classic characteristic of Indian peasant agriculture.     │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **2. Structural Unemployment**  │ A chronic, long-term mismatch between the **skills          │
│                                 │ possessed by job-seekers** and the **technical skills       │
│                                 │ demanded by employers**, driven by technological transitions│
│                                 │ or capital-intensive automation.                            │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

│    *(Keynesian Unemployment)*   │ the business cycle (economic recessions or depressions).    │
│                                 │ As aggregate demand drops, firms cut production and retrench│
│                                 │ staff (e.g., global tech layoffs during monetary tightening)│
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **4. Frictional Unemployment**  │ **Search Unemployment**: The temporary transition period    │
│                                 │ spent by a worker moving between jobs, or a fresh graduate  │
│                                 │ searching for their first employment. Normal and voluntary. │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **5. Seasonal Unemployment**    │ Unemployment occurring at predictable intervals of the year │
│                                 │ due to seasonal production cycles (e.g., agricultural slack │
│                                 │ seasons between sowing and harvesting, sugarcane crushing). │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **6. Vulnerable Employment**    │ Persons working as own-account workers or contributing family│
│                                 │ workers without formal employment contracts, paid leave,    │
│                                 │ or social security protections.                             │
└─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---b3:T84f,### 1. The Tripartite Employment Classification:
According to PLFS surveys, India's workforce is distributed across three structural categories:
* **Self-Employed (~57%)**:
  * *Own-account workers & employers*: Small shopkeepers, independent farmers, artisans (~38%).
  * *Unpaid Family Helpers*: Working in household family enterprises without explicit wage/remuneration (~19%).
* **Regular Wage / Salaried Workers (~21%)**:
  * Workers receiving fixed periodic wages/salaries; formal sector employees, government servants, organized corporate staff.
* **Casual Labour (~22%)**:
  * Daily wage earners in construction, seasonal agricultural laborers, brick-kiln workers; zero job security or contract.

---

### 2. The Informality Paradox:
* Approximately **88% to 90% of India's total workforce is informal**:
  * **Informal Sector Enterprises**: Unorganized micro-enterprises employing fewer than 10 workers without statutory registration.
  * **Informal Workers in Organized Sector**: Contractual, temporary, or outsourced workers in registered companies who do not receive written employment contracts, paid sick leave, or social security benefits (PF/ESI).

---

### 3. Female Labour Force Participation Rate (FLFPR) & The U-Shaped Curve:
* Historically, India's FLFPR was among the lowest in emerging economies (~23% in 2017–18).
* **The Claudia Goldin U-Shaped Hypothesis**:
  * In poor agrarian economies, female participation is high (necessity-driven subsistence farm work).
  * As household incomes rise and urbanization begins, female participation drops due to cultural norms, status signaling ("withdrawing women from manual labor as a mark of family prosperity"), and lack of safe urban jobs.
  * Only when women achieve higher tertiary education and white-collar opportunities expand does female participation rebound.
* **Recent Post-COVID Rise**: PLFS reports showed an increase in FLFPR (rising past 37%–41%), driven heavily by **rural women entering agriculture and self-help group micro-enterprises as unpaid family helpers** rather than a surge in high-productivity formal urban corporate jobs.

---b6:T61c,```
CARD 1: Explain the mathematical denominator difference between the Labour Force Participation Rate (LFPR) and the Unemployment Rate (UR).
ANSWER:
1. LFPR denominator: Total Working-Age Population (Ages 15+).
   LFPR = [Labour Force / Working-Age Population] * 100
2. UR denominator: ONLY the Labour Force (Employed + Unemployed).
   UR = [Unemployed / Labour Force] * 100
People who are not actively seeking employment (students, homemakers, discouraged workers)
are excluded from both the numerator and denominator of the Unemployment Rate.
```

```
CARD 2: What is Disguised Unemployment? In which sector of the Indian economy is it most prevalent, and what is the marginal productivity of labor in this state?
ANSWER:
1. Definition: A situation where more workers are employed in an activity than are technically
necessary to maintain output.
2. Marginal Productivity: MP_L = 0 (Withdrawing surplus laborers causes zero decline in total production).
3. Prevalent Sector: Indian Peasant Agriculture, where family members crowd onto fragmented land parcels.
```

```
CARD 3: What is the significant reform enacted regarding retrenchment thresholds under the Industrial Relations Code, 2020?
ANSWER:
The Industrial Relations Code, 2020 raised the threshold of employees requiring prior permission
from the appropriate government for layoffs, retrenchments, or commercial closures from 100 workers
to 300 workers. This grants industrial firms greater operational flexibility to adjust workforce
sizes according to market demand without bureaucratic delays.
```b7:Tf15,Poverty is fundamentally the inability of an individual or household to secure the minimum bundle of goods, services, and capabilities essential for human dignity and physical survival.

---

### Why India Measures Poverty via Consumption Expenditure (MPCE) Rather than Income:
In developed economies, poverty is assessed via reported tax returns and wage data. In India, official poverty estimation has historically utilized **Monthly Per Capita Consumption Expenditure (MPCE)** compiled by NSSO/NSO household consumer expenditure surveys:
1. **Pervasive Informality**: Approximately 90% of India's workforce is informal, characterized by cash transactions, self-employment, and irregular wages with zero tax filing.
2. **Agricultural Seasonality**: Rural incomes fluctuate violently between bumper harvests and monsoon droughts. Consumption expenditure is smoothed over time and reflects actual living standards far more accurately.
3. **Under-Reporting & Concealment**: Households systematically under-report income due to tax avoidance or suspicion, whereas daily consumption of food, kerosene, soap, and clothing is reported with higher fidelity.

---ba:T154f,Monetary poverty lines measure income/expenditure poverty, ignoring whether a family actually has clean drinking water, electricity, schooling, or sanitation. The **Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI)**, developed using the **Alkire-Foster (AF) methodology**, measures simultaneous overlapping deprivations.

### The Mathematical Formula of MPI:
The MPI is the product of two distinct metrics:

$$\mathbf{MPI = H \times A}$$

1. **Headcount Ratio ($H$)**: The proportion of the population that is multidimensionally poor:
   $$H = \frac{q}{N} \quad (\text{where } q = \text{number of multidimensionally poor individuals})$$
2. **Intensity of Poverty ($A$)**: The average proportion of weighted deprivation indicators experienced by the poor:
   $$A = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{q} c_i}{q} \quad (\text{where } c_i = \text{deprivation score of poor person } i)$$

> **Empirical Milestone**: As per NITI Aayog's National MPI Review, multidimensional poverty in India plunged from **29.17% in 2013–14 to 11.28% in 2022–23**, representing an unprecedented escape from multidimensional poverty for **24.8 crore citizens** (spearheaded by rural electrification, PM Ujjwala gas, Jal Jeevan Mission, and SBM sanitation).

---bb:Tf64,Economic inequality evaluates the disparity with which total national income, wealth, or consumption is shared across society.

### 1. The Lorenz Curve:
Originated by Max O. Lorenz (1905), it is a graphical representation of cumulative income distribution:
* **Horizontal Axis**: Cumulative percentage of population (ranked from poorest to richest).
* **Vertical Axis**: Cumulative percentage of total national income or wealth earned.
* **Line of Perfect Equality (45° Diagonal)**: Every decile of the population receives the exact same share of national income (e.g., 20% of people earn 20% of income).
* **The Lorenz Curve**: Always bows beneath the 45° line. The further it sags away from the diagonal, the higher the inequality in the economy.

```
Cumulative % of Income
        ▲
   100% ┼─────────────────────────────────────────────╭───┤ B
        │                                          ╭───
        │                                      ╭───    │
        │                           Area A ╭───        │
        │                              ╭───            │
        │                          ╭───                │
        │      Line of Equality╭───                    │
        │          (45°)   ╭───                        │
        │              ╭─── ╰──────                    │
        │          ╭───            ╰─── Lorenz Curve   │
        │      ╭───                    ╰───            │
        │  ╭───                Area B      ╰───        │
        └─┴────────────────────────────────────┴───────┴───► Cumulative % of Population
          0%                                  100%
```

---

### 2. The Gini Coefficient:
Formulated by Corrado Gini (1912), it quantifies the Lorenz Curve into a single mathematical index:

$$\mathbf{\text{Gini Coefficient}} = \frac{\mathbf{\text{Area } A}}{\mathbf{\text{Area } A + \text{Area } B}}$$

* **Gini = 0**: **Perfect Equality** (Lorenz curve coincides with the 45° line; every citizen has identical income).
* **Gini = 1 (or 100)**: **Perfect Inequality** (One single individual captures 100% of national income, while everyone else earns ₹0).
* **Wealth vs. Consumption Gini in India**:
  * **Consumption Expenditure Gini** in India is moderate (~**0.35 to 0.37**).
  * **Wealth Gini** in India is extreme (~**0.82 to 0.84**), demonstrating massive concentration of physical capital, real estate, and equity ownership among the top decile.

---

### 3. Alternative Distributional Indicators:

| Distribution Metric | Mathematical Formulation | Epistemic Interpretation & Significance |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Palma Ratio** | $$\text{Palma Ratio} = \frac{\text{Income Share of Richest 10\%}}{\text{Income Share of Poorest 40\%}}$$ | Gabriel Palma observed that middle-class (deciles 5 to 9) capture ~50% of income across all societies; inequality is driven almost entirely by the tug-of-war between top 10% and bottom 40%. Palma $> 1$ indicates significant disparity. |
| **Poverty Gap Index (PGI)** | $$\text{PGI} = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{q} \left(\frac{z - y_i}{z}\right)$$ | Measures the **depth of poverty** (the average cash shortfall of poor individuals below the poverty line $z$ as a share of $z$). Reflects how much resources are needed to eliminate poverty. |
| **Squared Poverty Gap (SPG)** *(Poverty Severity Index)* | Squares the poverty gap of each poor individual before averaging: $\frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{q} \left(\frac{z - y_i}{z}\right)^2$ | Gives disproportionate mathematical weight to the poorest of the poor (measures **severity of deprivation**). |

---bd:T701,```
CARD 1: Why did the Suresh Tendulkar Committee abandon calorie norms for estimating poverty in India?
ANSWER:
Tendulkar observed that calorie consumption was steadily declining across all expenditure classes
even as incomes and real living standards were rising (the "calorie consumption puzzle").
Anchoring poverty solely to calories would have absurdly shown rising poverty during rapid economic
growth. Therefore, Tendulkar decoupled poverty from calories, anchored it to urban living standards,
and incorporated explicit spending on health and education under MRP.
```

```
CARD 2: Contrast the Uniform Reference Period (URP), Mixed Reference Period (MRP), and Modified Mixed Reference Period (MMRP).
ANSWER:
1. URP: 30-day recall for ALL goods and services.
2. MRP: 365-day recall for 5 infrequent non-food items (clothing, footwear, durables, education,
institutional medical); 30-day recall for all remaining food/non-food items.
3. MMRP: 7-day recall for high-frequency perishable food items (edible oil, eggs, fish, meat,
vegetables, fruits, spices); 365-day recall for 5 infrequent non-food items; 30-day recall for rest.
```

```
CARD 3: Define the Lorenz Curve and the Gini Coefficient. How is the Gini Coefficient mathematically derived from the Lorenz Curve?
ANSWER:
1. Lorenz Curve: A graphical plot of cumulative percentage of population against cumulative
percentage of national income/wealth received, compared against the 45-degree Line of Perfect Equality.
2. Gini Coefficient: A mathematical measure of inequality derived as the ratio:
   Gini = Area A / (Area A + Area B)
   where Area A is the area between the 45-degree line and the Lorenz Curve, and Area B is the area
   underneath the Lorenz Curve. Ranges from 0 (perfect equality) to 1 (perfect inequality).
```be:T70a,The **Balance of Payments (BoP)** is a systematic statistical record of all economic transactions conducted between the **residents of a country** and the **rest of the world** during a specified accounting interval (quarterly or annually).

### The Double-Entry Bookkeeping Framework:
Every international transaction is recorded as both a credit and a debit entry:
* **Credit Entry ($+$ / Inflow)**: Any transaction that leads to an **inflow of foreign exchange into the domestic economy** (e.g., merchandise exports, inward software remittances, foreign direct investment received, sovereign loans raised from abroad).
* **Debit Entry ($-$ / Outflow)**: Any transaction that results in an **outflow of foreign exchange to the rest of the world** (e.g., merchandise imports, outward tourism expenditures, dividend remittances to foreign shareholders, principal repayment on external debt).

```
                            THE FUNDAMENTAL ACCOUNTING IDENTITY
      ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │  Current Account + Capital/Financial Account + Errors & Omissions ≡ 0       │
      └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

> **The Epistemic Truism**: In an accounting sense, **the Balance of Payments ALWAYS balances** ($= 0$). When economists say "India has a BoP deficit", they refer strictly to the **Overall Balance** before accommodating central bank reserve operations!

---c0:T74f,The Current Account records transactions that do not create future sovereign liabilities or claim redemptions:

| Component | Mathematical / Economic Formulation | Nature of Inflows & Indian Reality |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **1. Merchandise Trade Balance (Goods)** | $$\text{Trade Balance} = \text{Merchandise Exports} - \text{Merchandise Imports}$$ | • **Structurally in heavy deficit for India** (-\$150B to -\$250B annually) due to high import dependence on:<br/>- Crude oil and gas (~85% import dependent)<br/>- Electronic hardware and components<br/>- Gold, precious stones, and capital machinery. |
| **2. Services (Invisibles)** | Net Service Receipts | • **Structurally in large surplus for India** (+\$100B to +\$150B annually).<br/>• Dominated by Software, IT services, BPO/BPM, Global Capability Centres (GCCs), transport, and consultancy. |
| **3. Primary Income (Investment Income)** | Factor Income Balance | • Flow of factor income (Interest, Dividends, Profits, and Compensation of Employees).<br/>• **Structurally in deficit for India**: Dividend/profit outflows on foreign capital invested in India far exceed returns earned by Indians investing abroad. |
| **4. Secondary Income (Current Transfers)** | Unilateral Transfers | • **Unrequited Transfers** (no economic quid pro quo): Private cross-border remittances, gifts, grants.<br/>• **Massive surplus for India**: India is the **world's largest remittance recipient** (> \$110 Billion annually, driven by GCC nations, USA, and UK diaspora). |

$$\mathbf{\text{Current Account Balance (CAB)}} = \text{Merchandise Trade Balance} + \mathbf{\text{Net Invisibles}} \; (\text{Services} + \text{Primary} + \text{Secondary})$$

$$\mathbf{\text{Current Account Deficit (CAD)}} = \text{When CAB is Negative} \quad \Big[\text{CAD} \le 2.5\% \text{ of GDP is regarded as sustainable}\Big]$$

---

### 2. External Borrowings: Commercial vs. Concessional:
* **External Commercial Borrowings (ECBs)**:
  * Commercial loans, bonds, or supplier credits raised by eligible Indian corporate entities from recognized non-resident lenders.
  * Regulated strictly by the RBI via **All-in-Cost Ceilings** (benchmark rate $+$ margin) and **Minimum Average Maturity Periods (MAMP)** (typically 3 to 5 years) to prevent corporate over-indebtedness.
* **External Assistance (Sovereign Concessional Loans)**:
  * Multilateral and bilateral loans raised by the Government of India from the World Bank, Asian Development Bank (ADB), JICA (Japan), and KfW (Germany) featuring low interest rates and long grace/amortization periods.

---

---c2:T9fd,```
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Autonomous Transactions                       │ Accommodating Transactions                    │
│ ("Above the Line" Items)                      │ ("Below the Line" Items)                      │

│   (e.g., firms exporting goods, tourists      │   gap or absorb the surplus** resulting from  │
│   traveling, investors seeking stock returns).│   autonomous transactions.                    │
│ • Conducted completely independent of the     │ • Include **drawing down or accumulating      │
│   state of the Balance of Payments.           │   Foreign Exchange Reserves** or borrowing    │
│ • Determine whether the BoP is in deficit or  │   from the IMF.                               │

```

$$\mathbf{\text{Overall BoP Balance}} = \mathbf{\text{Current Account Balance}} + \mathbf{\text{Net Capital Inflows}} + \mathbf{\text{Errors \& Omissions}}$$

* **If Overall BoP Balance is Positive ($> 0$)**: India has a BoP Surplus. The RBI purchases the excess foreign currency from the market, leading to an **Accumulation of Forex Reserves** (recorded with a negative sign in the official reserve account to balance the books).
* **If Overall BoP Balance is Negative ($< 0$)**: India has a BoP Deficit. The RBI sells US dollars from its vaults to meet market demand, leading to a **Drawdown of Forex Reserves**.

---c3:T6af,Managed by the Reserve Bank of India under the legal provisions of the *Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934* and *Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), 1999*:

```
                                INDIA'S FOREX RESERVES
                                           │
  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────────┴───────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  ▼                       ▼                                        ▼                       ▼
[FOREIGN CURRENCY]     [GOLD RESERVES]                          [SDRs]                  [RTP]
Assets (FCA)           Held in RBI vaults &                     Special Drawing         Reserve Tranche
US$, Euro, Yen, GBP    Bank of England vaults                   Rights allocated by IMF Position with IMF
(~85-88% of total)     (~8-10% of total)                        (~2-3% of total)        (~0.5-1% of total)
```

1. **Foreign Currency Assets (FCA)**: High-quality multicurrency sovereign treasury bonds and bank deposits denominated in US Dollars, Euros, Pound Sterling, Japanese Yen. (Movements in dollar exchange rate cause valuation changes in non-dollar assets).
2. **Gold**: Physical gold bullion held by the RBI at Nagpur vaults and with the Bank of England/BIS.
3. **Special Drawing Rights (SDR)**: International reserve assets allocated by the IMF based on member quotas.
4. **Reserve Tranche Position (RTP)**: The unconditional liquidity line an IMF member country can draw from the IMF without conditionality (portion of quota paid in reserve currencies).

---c4:T42a,| Vulnerability Metric | Mathematical Formulation | Benchmark Prudential Threshold & Reality |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Import Cover** | $$\text{Import Cover} = \frac{\text{Forex Reserves}}{\text{Monthly Merchandise Imports}}$$ | Expressed in number of months. India maintains ~**10 to 12 months** of import cover (safe threshold > 6 months). |
| **Debt Service Ratio** | $$\text{Debt Service Ratio} = \frac{\text{Principal Repayment} + \text{Interest Due}}{\text{Current Account Receipts}} \times 100$$ | Measures capacity to service external debt using export & invisible earnings. India's ratio is healthy at ~**5%–6%**. |
| **External Debt to GDP** | Total External Debt / GDP | India's sovereign and commercial external debt ratio is ~**18.5% to 19.0% of GDP**, among the lowest among major emerging market economies. |
| **Short-Term Debt to Forex** | Short-Term Debt / Forex Reserves | Short-term debt (by original maturity) as a percentage of total Forex reserves stands at ~**20%–22%**, confirming exceptional foreign exchange liquidity buffer. |

---c6:T6f3,```
CARD 1: Explain the distinction between Autonomous and Accommodating transactions in the Balance of Payments.
ANSWER:
1. Autonomous Transactions ("Above the Line"): Carried out for independent commercial motives
(e.g., maximizing profit, export-import trade, foreign direct investment), independent of the
state of the BoP.
2. Accommodating Transactions ("Below the Line"): Carried out by the central bank deliberately
to bridge the deficit or absorb the surplus generated by autonomous transactions (e.g., drawing down
or building foreign exchange reserves, IMF standby facilities).
```

```
CARD 2: Detail the Arvind Mayaram Committee criteria used in India to distinguish Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) from Foreign Portfolio Investment (FPI).
ANSWER:
1. Listed Companies: An investment of 10% or more of the paid-up equity capital by a single foreign
investor/group is classified as FDI. Any investment below 10% is classified as FPI.
2. Unlisted Companies: ANY investment in an unlisted company, regardless of percentage, is classified as FDI.
3. Nature: FDI brings lasting interest, managerial control, and strategic tech transfer; FPI is
liquid, financial market investment seeking portfolio returns without management control.
```

```
CARD 3: State the four constitutional components of India's Foreign Exchange Reserves in descending order of size.
ANSWER:
1. Foreign Currency Assets (FCA): Foreign sovereign treasury securities, central bank deposits (~85-88%).
2. Gold Reserves: Physical gold bullion held at RBI vaults and Bank of England (~8-10%).
3. Special Drawing Rights (SDRs): Reserve assets allocated by the International Monetary Fund (~2-3%).
4. Reserve Tranche Position (RTP): India's unconditional credit liquidity tranche with the IMF (~0.5-1%).
```c7:T102b,An exchange rate is the price of one sovereign currency expressed in terms of another currency. The institutional mechanism by which this price is established determines the exchange rate regime:

---

### The Evolution of India's Exchange Rate Architecture:
1. **Pre-1991 Era (Fixed Peg)**: The Indian Rupee was pegged officially to the British Pound Sterling (and later to a discretionary basket of currencies). Devaluations required executive cabinet declarations (e.g., 1966 devaluation and the two-step July 1991 devaluation).
2. **Liberalised Exchange Rate Management System (LERMS, March 1992)**:
   * A **dual-rate transitional system**: Exporters surrendered 40% of their foreign exchange earnings to the RBI at an official, fixed rate (used by the government to import essential crude oil and fertilizer).
   * The remaining 60% was sold in the open market at market-determined floating rates.
3. **Unified Exchange Rate System (UERS, March 1993 to Present)**:
   * The dual-rate mechanism was scrapped; the Rupee was fully unified on a **market-determined managed floating exchange rate regime**.

---c8:T115d,Tracking bilateral exchange rates (e.g., Rupee vs. US Dollar alone) provides a distorted picture because India conducts massive trade with China, the European Union, UAE, Saudi Arabia, Japan, and the UK. To evaluate overall currency competitiveness, the RBI computes effective basket indices:

```
                            THE EFFECTIVE EXCHANGE RATE BASKETS
                                             │
             ┌───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┐
             ▼                                                               ▼
   [NOMINAL EFFECTIVE EXCHANGE                     [REAL EFFECTIVE EXCHANGE
          RATE (NEER)]                                    RATE (REER)]
   • Weighted geometric average of                • NEER adjusted for inflation
     bilateral nominal exchange rates               differentials between India and
     against trade partners' currencies.            its 40 trading partners.
   • Measures multilateral nominal value.         • The definitive measure of global
                                                    **trade competitiveness**!
```

---

### The RBI 40-Currency Basket (Base Year: 2015–16 = 100)
In December 2020, the Reserve Bank overhauled its official effective exchange rate series:
* Expanded and recalibrated from the earlier 36-currency basket (Base 2004–05) to a modern **40-currency basket (Base Year 2015–16 = 100)** to incorporate dynamic trade patterns (expanded weights for China, ASEAN, and Gulf economies).
* Uses 3-year moving average trade weights (covering both exports and imports).

---

### Mathematical Interpretation of REER Index Movements:

$$\mathbf{\text{REER Index Benchmark}} = \mathbf{100} \quad (\text{Base Year Equilibrium)}$$

```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ REER Index Value                │ Economic Status & Trade Repercussions                       │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **$\mathbf{\text{REER} > 100}$**│ **Currency is OVERVALUED**:                                 │
│ *(e.g., REER = 106)*            │ • The Rupee has appreciated in real terms relative to       │
│                                 │   trading partners after accounting for inflation.          │
│                                 │ • **Indian exports become more expensive and less           │
│                                 │   competitive globally**.                                   │
│                                 │ • Foreign imports become artificially cheaper for Indians.  │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **$\mathbf{\text{REER} < 100}$**│ **Currency is UNDERVALUED**:                                │
│ *(e.g., REER = 94)*             │ • The Rupee has depreciated in real terms.                  │
│                                 │ • **Indian exports gain aggressive price competitiveness**  │
│                                 │   in global markets.                                        │
│                                 │ • Foreign imports become more expensive, promoting domestic │
│                                 │   import substitution.                                      │
└─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---c9:Tb4d,Currency convertibility refers to the sovereign freedom granted to residents and non-residents to freely convert the domestic currency into foreign currencies (and vice versa) at market-clearing rates without administrative permits or quantitative quotas.

```
                                  CURRENCY CONVERTIBILITY
                                             │
             ┌───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┐
             ▼                                                               ▼
[CURRENT ACCOUNT CONVERTIBILITY (CAC)]                         [CAPITAL ACCOUNT CONVERTIBILITY (FCAC)]
• Converting currency for trade in goods,                     • Converting currency for buying foreign
  services, travel, education, medical,                         stocks, bonds, real estate, acquiring
  and inward/outward factor remittances.                        overseas firms, or taking foreign debt.
• **100% FULLY CONVERTIBLE IN INDIA (Since 1994)**!            • **PARTIALLY CONVERTIBLE IN INDIA**.
```

---

### 1. Full Current Account Convertibility (August 1994)
* Under **Article VIII of the International Monetary Fund (IMF) Articles of Agreement**, member states pledge not to impose restrictions on the making of payments and transfers for current international transactions.
* On **19th August 1994**, India officially accepted Article VIII obligations, granting **Full Current Account Convertibility**.
* **Statutory Framework**: Governed by the *Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), 1999* (which replaced the draconian criminal statute *Foreign Exchange Regulation Act - FERA, 1973*).
* **The Liberalised Remittance Scheme (LRS)**:
  * Established by the RBI in 2004.
  * Allows all resident Indian individuals (including minors) to freely remit up to **\$250,000 per financial year** for permissible current and capital account transactions (education abroad, travel, gift, medical, or purchasing foreign equities/property).

---

### 2. Capital Account Convertibility: The Indian Approach
Unlike the current account, India has followed a path of **gradual, calibrated, and partial capital account convertibility**:
* **Where India is Open**:
  * Inward FDI (100% automatic route across almost all industrial sectors).
  * FPI investments in Indian equity and debt markets (subject to aggregate portfolio caps and Fully Accessible Route - FAR in sovereign G-Secs).
* **Where India Maintains Strict Capital Controls**:
  * Outward debt investments by domestic retail citizens (capped under LRS).
  * External Commercial Borrowings (ECBs) capped with strict tenure, use-case, and interest spread limitations.
  * Prohibition on foreign investors trading in domestic rupee commodity derivatives.

---ca:T1333,The Reserve Bank constituted two historic expert committees under the chairmanship of former RBI Deputy Governor **S.S. Tarapore**:
1. **Tarapore Committee I (1997)**: Recommended a 3-year phased roadmap towards Full Capital Account Convertibility (FCAC). The roadmap was paused following the devastating 1997 East Asian Financial Crisis.
2. **Tarapore Committee II (2006)**: Re-evaluated the framework and reiterated that Full Convertibility should be a gradual destination contingent upon fulfilling strict macroeconomic preconditions.

---

### The Tarapore Pre-conditions for Full Capital Account Convertibility:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Precondition Dimension          │ Precise Quantitative Benchmark Mandated                     │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Fiscal Consolidation**     │ Gross Fiscal Deficit of the Central Government must be      │
│                                 │ reduced and anchored at **$\le 3.5\%$ (or $3.0\%$) of GDP**,│
│                                 │ accompanied by a reduction in total public debt ratios.     │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **2. Inflation Mandate**        │ A track record of low and stable domestic inflation anchored│
│                                 │ within a **3% to 5% band** over a multi-year horizon.       │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **3. Banking Sector Health**    │ • **Gross NPAs of commercial banks must be below 5%**       │
│                                 │   (Tarapore II recommended Net NPAs $\le 3\%$).             │
│                                 │ • Deregulation of interest rates and elimination of CRR/SLR │
│                                 │   statutory pre-emptions on banking liquidity.              │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **4. Foreign Exchange Buffer**  │ • Sufficient foreign currency reserves to cover **at least  │
│                                 │   6 months of imports**.                                    │
│                                 │ • Forex reserves must comfortably exceed the country's      │
│                                 │   short-term external debt and currency liabilities.        │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **5. Sustainable CAD**          │ The Current Account Deficit must remain within a prudent,   │
│                                 │ non-volatile corridor of **$\le 2.0\%$ of GDP**.            │
└─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

> **Why India Avoids Premature Full Capital Account Convertibility**:  
> The 1997 East Asian Tiger collapse (Thailand, Indonesia, South Korea) proved that opening capital accounts before strengthening domestic banks allows massive foreign speculative capital ("hot money") to flood the banking sector, inflate asset bubbles, and instantly flee during a shock. Capital flight causes catastrophic currency collapse, bank runs, and macroeconomic devastation. India's partial convertibility insulated it from the 1997 Asian crisis and the 2008 Global Financial Meltdown.

---cb:Ted3,Formulated by economists Robert Mundell and Marcus Fleming, the **Trilemma** states that an open macroeconomic economy **cannot simultaneously achieve all three desirable policy goals**:

```
                                  THE IMPOSSIBLE TRINITY
                                            ▲
                                           / \
                                          /   \
                                         /  1  \
                                        /       \
                      [FIXED EXCHANGE] ─────────── [FREE CAPITAL]
                            RATE                     MOBILITY
                               \                 /
                                \       3       /
                                 \             /
                                  \     2     /
                                   \         /
                                    ▼       ▼
                             [INDEPENDENT MONETARY]
                                     POLICY
```

```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Trilemma Pair Chosen            │ Inevitable Trade-Off / Abandoned Goal                       │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Option 1: Fixed Exchange Rate │ Must **ABANDON Independent Monetary Policy**!               │
│ + Free Capital Mobility**       │ Domestic interest rates are held hostage to foreign central │
│ *(e.g., Hong Kong, Eurozone)*   │ bank policy to maintain the peg. Cannot cut rates in slump. │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Option 2: Independent Policy  │ Must **ABANDON Fixed Exchange Rate (Float Currency)**!       │
│ + Free Capital Mobility**       │ Central bank freely sets policy repo rates to manage        │
│ *(e.g., USA, UK, Japan)*        │ domestic growth/inflation, but currency fluctuates wildly.  │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Option 3: Fixed Exchange Rate │ Must **ABANDON Free Capital Mobility (Capital Controls)**!   │
│ + Independent Monetary Policy** │ Allows sovereign interest rate control and stable exchange  │
│ *(e.g., China historically)*    │ rate, but requires rigid administrative bans on capital flow│
└─────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

* **India's Intermediate Position**: India operates an "Intermediate Trilemma" regime: a **managed floating exchange rate**, **substantial (but not full) capital mobility**, and a **sovereign independent inflation-targeting monetary policy**.

---cc:T18b9,Released by the Directorate General of Foreign Trade (DGFT), Ministry of Commerce and Industry, the **Foreign Trade Policy 2023** took effect on **April 1, 2023**. Unlike previous Five-Year FTP iterations (e.g., FTP 2015–20), FTP 2023 is a **dynamic, perpetual policy document** without a fixed sunset date, designed to be nimble and continuously responsive to shifting global trade realities.

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                   FOREIGN TRADE POLICY 2023: THE FOUR STRATEGIC PILLARS                          │
├────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Strategic Pillar   │ Operational Mechanisms & Institutional Commitments                          │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. Incentive to  │ Structural shift away from export subsidies (actionable under WTO SCM       │
│    Remission**     │ rules) toward automatic, transparent remission of embedded domestic taxes:  │
│                    │ • **RoDTEP** (Remission of Duties and Taxes on Exported Products)           │
│                    │ • **RoSCTL** (Rebate of State and Central Taxes and Levies for textiles)    │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **2. Export Promo- │ Institutionalizing a decentralized export ecosystem:                        │
│    tion through    │ • **Districts as Export Hubs (DEH)**: Each district identifies 2–3 products │
│    Collaboration** │   with competitive advantage; District Export Promotion Committees (DEPC).  │
│                    │ • Active coordination with Indian Foreign Missions abroad for market access.│
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **3. Ease of Doing │ Drastic reduction in transaction costs and regulatory friction:             │
│    Business**      │ • MSME user charges for Advance Authorisation and EPCG schemes capped at    │
│                    │   ₹5,000 (down from ₹1 Lakh). Paperless dual-use electronic applications.   │
├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **4. Emerging      │ Future-proofing India's export basket:                                      │
│    Areas**         │ • **E-Commerce Exports**: Consignment cap doubled from ₹5 Lakh to ₹10 Lakh; │
│                    │   establishment of designated E-Commerce Export Hubs (ECEH).                │
│                    │ • **SCOMET Streamlining**: Rationalized export of dual-use high-tech items. │
│                    │ • **Green Technology**: Reduced export obligation for green energy goods.   │
└────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### The $2 Trillion Export Target by 2030
FTP 2023 establishes a national target of achieving **US$ 2 Trillion in aggregate exports by 2030**:
- **US$ 1 Trillion** in Merchandise Exports
- **US$ 1 Trillion** in Services Exports (leveraging India's global IT, BPM, financial, and engineering consultancy leadership).

### International Trade Settlement in Indian Rupee (INR)
Under the RBI framework dated **July 11, 2022**, and codified within FTP 2023, India operationalized the mechanism for invoicing, payment, and settlement of international trade in Indian Rupees:
- **Special Rupee Vostro Accounts (SVRA)**: Foreign correspondent banks open SVRAs with authorized dealer banks in India.
- **Settlement Mechanics**: Indian importers pay in INR into the foreign bank's SVRA; Indian exporters are paid in INR from the foreign bank's SVRA.
- **Surplus Balances**: Foreign entities can invest surplus INR balances in Indian Government Securities (G-Secs), Treasury Bills, or local investment projects.
- **Strategic Impact**: Bypasses US Dollar payment gateways (SWIFT sanctions), insulates bilateral trade from exchange rate volatility, preserves forex reserves, and marks the initial step toward the internationalization of the Rupee.

### Towns of Export Excellence (TEE) & Status Holders
- **Towns of Export Excellence**: FTP 2023 added 4 new towns—**Faridabad** (Apparel), **Mirzapur** (Handmade Carpets), **Moradabad** (Handicrafts), and **Varanasi** (Handlooms and Handicrafts)—taking the national total to **43 TEEs** eligible for financial support under the Market Access Initiative (MAI).
- **Status Holder Tiers**: Recalibrated export thresholds (1-Star at $3M up to 5-Star at $800M) allowing exporters priority customs clearances, exemption from compulsory negotiation of documents, and two-star status holders to self-certify manufactured goods.

---ce:T73e,```
CARD 1: Define the Real Effective Exchange Rate (REER) and explain what an index value of 108 indicates about export competitiveness.
ANSWER:
1. REER is the trade-weighted geometric average of bilateral nominal exchange rates of the domestic
currency against a basket of major trading partner currencies (RBI uses 40 currencies),
adjusted for relative inflation differentials.
2. A REER reading of 108 (> 100) indicates that the domestic currency is OVERVALUED by approximately
8% relative to its trade partners. Consequently, domestic manufactured exports are less price-competitive
in international markets, while foreign imports become cheaper.
```

```
CARD 2: What is the Mundell-Fleming Trilemma (The Impossible Trinity)? State the three policy goals and explain why a country cannot achieve all three simultaneously.
ANSWER:
1. The Three Goals: (a) Fixed / Stable Exchange Rate; (b) Free Capital Mobility; (c) Independent Monetary Policy.
2. Why Impossible: If an economy has free capital mobility and fixes its exchange rate, any independent
interest rate change by the central bank will trigger massive arbitrage capital flows that break the peg.
To defend the peg, the central bank must match foreign interest rates, surrendering domestic monetary policy autonomy.
```

```
CARD 3: Detail the three key macroeconomic preconditions recommended by the Tarapore Committees before India can transition to Full Capital Account Convertibility.
ANSWER:
1. Fiscal Prudence: Gross Fiscal Deficit of the Centre anchored at <= 3.5% (or 3.0%) of GDP.
2. Banking Resilience: Gross NPAs of commercial banks reduced to below 5% (Net NPAs <= 3%),
supported by adequate capital adequacy ratios.
3. Macroeconomic Stability: Inflation track record contained within a stable 3% to 5% band,
supported by forex reserves covering at least 6 months of imports.
```cf:Tea7,In July 1944, delegates from 44 Allied nations convened at the Mount Washington Hotel in Bretton Woods, New Hampshire, USA, to construct a new multilateral financial architecture designed to prevent the competitive currency devaluations, protectionist tariff walls, and macroeconomic chaos that catalyzed the Great Depression and World War II.

### The Twin Institutions:
1. **The International Monetary Fund (IMF)**: Mandated to maintain **global macroeconomic and monetary stability**, oversee the international monetary system, and provide short-to-medium-term balance of payments (BoP) emergency financing.
2. **The International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD / World Bank)**: Mandated to finance the **long-term post-war reconstruction of war-torn Europe** and promote capital-intensive infrastructure and economic development in emerging nations.

```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Feature                         │ International Monetary Fund     │ World Bank (IBRD & IDA)         │
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
│ **Core Functional Mandate**     │ **Macroeconomic Stability &     │ **Long-Term Economic            │
│                                 │ Balance of Payments Support**.  │ Development & Poverty Alleviation│
├─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤

---d0:T100f,### 1. Quotas and Voting Power
Every member country is assigned an official **Quota** upon joining, which forms the bedrock of its financial and institutional relationship with the IMF:
* **Determinants of Quota**: A weighted formula based on:
  * **GDP (50%)**: Measured 60% by market exchange rates and 40% by PPP.
  * **Openness (30%)**: Annual ratio of current payments and receipts to GDP.
  * **Economic Variability (15%)**: Volatility of current receipts and net capital flows.
  * **International Reserves (5%)**.
* **Threefold Function of Quotas**:
  1. **Subscription**: Determines the financial contribution the member must deposit into the Fund.
  2. **Voting Power**: Each member receives 250 basic votes $+$ 1 additional vote per SDR 100,000 of quota.
  3. **Access to Financing**: Determines the maximum credit volume a member can borrow.
* **The 85% Supermajority & The US Veto**: Major structural decisions (amending Articles of Agreement, quota revisions) require an **85% supermajority**. The United States holds **~16.5% of total voting power**, granting Washington an exclusive, unilateral **statutory veto** over the IMF!
* **India's Position**: India holds a **Quota of ~2.75%** and a **Voting Share of ~2.63%**, ranking as the **8th largest member**.

---

### 2. Special Drawing Rights (SDRs) - "Paper Gold"
Created by the IMF in **1969** under the First Amendment to the Articles of Agreement as a supplementary international reserve asset:
* **Legal Nature**: The SDR is **NEITHER a currency NOR a direct claim on the IMF**. It represents a potential claim on the freely usable currencies of IMF members.
* **Allocation**: Allocated to member countries in proportion to their IMF quotas (e.g., historic general allocation of \$650 Billion in August 2021 post-COVID).
* **The SDR Currency Valuation Basket (5 Currencies)**:
  * Reviewed every 5 years by the IMF Executive Board:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Basket Currency                 │ Current Weight │ Year of Inclusion / Benchmark Status       │
├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ **1. US Dollar (USD)**          │ **43.38%**     │ Core global reserve asset                  │
│ **2. Euro (EUR)**               │ **29.31%**     │ Replaced Deutsche Mark & French Franc 1999 │
│ **3. Chinese Renminbi (RMB)**   │ **12.28%**     │ **Included in 2016** (Major reform)        │
│ **4. Japanese Yen (JPY)**       │ **7.59%**      │ Long-standing reserve currency             │
│ **5. British Pound (GBP)**      │ **7.44%**      │ Long-standing reserve currency             │
└─────────────────────────────────┴────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---

### 3. IMF Lending Facilities & Flagship Publications
* **Stand-By Arrangement (SBA)**: Short-term balance of payments support (12–36 months).
* **Extended Fund Facility (EFF)**: Medium-term assistance for countries facing structural balance of payments crises requiring profound institutional reforms (used by **India during the July 1991 crisis** to anchor the LPG reforms).
* **Poverty Reduction and Growth Trust (PRGT)**: Concessional zero-interest emergency financing for low-income countries.
* **Flagship IMF Reports**:
  1. *World Economic Outlook (WEO)* (Biannual global growth projections).
  2. *Global Financial Stability Report (GFSR)*.
  3. *Fiscal Monitor*.
